Everything is just... slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, not at all. Like I was trying to explain, it's just the opposite. I don't have the energy to fidget. I'm just... stuck. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it feels exactly like I'm moving through mud. Mud... you know, they say mud baths are supposed to be good for your skin. My wife went to a spa once for her 40th birthday, and she came back smelling like sulfur. It was awful. She said it was relaxing, but I don't see how sitting in a tub of hot dirt could be relaxing. The spa was out in Sedona, Arizona. Have you ever been out there? The red rocks are beautiful, especially when the sun sets. We took a jeep tour while we were there, and the driver was this young kid, maybe twenty-two, who kept telling us all these conspiracy theories about aliens living under the mountains. He had this big bumper sticker on the back of the jeep that said \"The Truth is Out There.\" Reminded me of that old TV show, The X-Files. I used to watch that every Sunday night. The theme song always gave me the creeps. I tried to find it on streaming the other day, but there are so many streaming services now, it's impossible to keep track. You have to pay ten bucks here, fifteen bucks there, and pretty soon you're paying more than you did for cable. Cable used to be so simple. You just turned on the TV and flipped through the channels with a clicker... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Fidgeting? Right, no. I don't fidget. It's just... everything is so slow. Even when people ask me things, I hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. It's like my brain is stuck in the mud, too.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often these thoughts of wanting to be dead occur in a typical day?\n\nPatient: How often? Um... it's a lot. The thought just keeps coming that I should just be dead. Dead... you know, we drove past the old cemetery on Elm Street the other day. My wife was driving. My grandfather had an oak tree in his front yard. We used to rake the leaves into these massive piles in the fall and jump in them. Fall used to be my favorite season. The air gets so crisp, and you can finally wear a good sweater. I have this one heavy wool sweater my wife bought me in Ireland. We went there for our 20th anniversary. The flight was so long, I couldn't sleep at all. I tried to watch one of those movies they play on the little screen on the back of the seat, but the headphone jack was broken. It just kept cutting out. They don't even give you real headphones on planes anymore, just those cheap plastic earbuds that hurt your ears. My son goes through a pair of earbuds every month. He just leaves them in his pockets and they go right through the washing machine. You'd think he'd learn by now, but kids just don't pay attention to things... \n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? How often... right. Yeah, the thought just keeps coming that I should just be dead. And when I'm alone in the house, it really scares me. I sit there and I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't pace. I don't have the energy to pace. Like I've been trying to say, it's... it's all just so slow. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like my body is moving through mud. Mud... you know, my boots got covered in mud the last time it rained. I left them on the back porch and they're probably still out there, all caked in it. I bought those boots at a surplus store a few years ago. They're military grade, really heavy duty. The guy at the store said they'd last a lifetime. He was an older guy, had a patch on his jacket from the Vietnam War. My uncle served in Vietnam. He never really talked about it much, just said it was hot and rained all the time. Rain always makes my joints ache, especially my left knee. I tore a ligament playing softball in my thirties. We had a company team, the \"B-Shift Bombers.\" We weren't very good, but we always went out for pizza and beer after the games. There was this place called Luigi's that made the best deep dish. The crust was so thick you had to eat it with a knife and fork. I tried making pizza at home once, bought the dough and everything, but the oven got too hot and I burned the bottom black. The smoke alarm went off and scared the cat half to death. The cat hid under the sofa for three days. It's funny how animals react to loud noises...\n\nWait, pacing? No, no constant motion. I don't feel like moving at all. It's just... I'm moving through mud. And it's the same with talking. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it feels exactly like I'm moving through mud. Mud... you know, they say mud baths are supposed to be good for your skin. My wife went to a spa once for her 40th birthday, and she came back smelling like sulfur. It was awful. She said it was relaxing, but I don't see how sitting in a tub of hot dirt could be relaxing. My grandfather had an oak tree in his front yard. We used to rake the leaves into these massive piles in the fall and jump in them. Fall used to be my favorite season. The air gets so crisp, and you can finally wear a good sweater. I have this one heavy wool sweater my wife bought me in Ireland. We went there for our 20th anniversary. The flight was so long, I couldn't sleep at all. I tried to watch one of those movies they play on the little screen on the back of the seat, but the headphone jack was broken. It just kept cutting out. They don't even give you real headphones on planes anymore, just those cheap plastic earbuds that hurt your ears. My son goes through a pair of earbuds every month. I bought those boots at a surplus store a few years ago. They're military grade, really heavy duty. The guy at the store said they'd last a lifetime. Rain always makes my joints ache, especially my left knee. I tore a ligament playing softball in my thirties. The smoke alarm went off and scared the cat half to death. The cat hid under the sofa for three days. It's funny how animals react to loud noises...\n\nWait, pacing? No, no constant motion. I don't feel like moving at all. It's just... I'm moving through mud. And it's the same with talking. You ask me a question, and I hear it, I hear you perfectly fine, but... the words just won't come. It takes me a long time to get the words out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless? No... no, I don't feel restless. Like I keep trying to explain, I don't want to move at all. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it's just like my body is moving through mud. Mud... you know, my neighbor's dog tracked mud all over their new white carpets last week. It's a Golden Retriever, beautiful dog, but completely untrained. They just let him run loose in the yard, and he digs these massive holes near the fence line. I told them they should put up some chicken wire, but they didn't listen. People don't listen to advice anymore. Back when I worked at the hardware store, people used to come in all the time asking for advice on home projects. I'd spend twenty minutes explaining how to fix a leaky pipe or patch drywall. Drywall dust gets everywhere, you know. It coats your lungs if you don't wear a mask. I always wore one of those paper masks, the ones with the little metal strip you pinch over your nose. They always fogged up my glasses, though. I've worn glasses since I was twelve. I'm nearsighted. Without them, everything past my arm is just a big blur. I tried contacts once in my twenties, but I could never get them in my eyes right. Just the thought of touching my eyeball makes me cringe...

Wait, what was the question? Restless? Right, no. I'm not restless. I'm just... stuck in the mud. Even talking is hard. When people ask me something, I can hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. It's all just so slow.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I guess what brought me in is I’m just so worn out. I mean, just walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day long. \n\nHauling lumber... my older brother actually used to work at a lumber yard up in Oregon. We went up to see him back in, gosh, maybe '98? Or '99. It rained the entire week we were there. He drove this beat-up old truck that smelled exactly like wet dog and pine sap. I can't stand the smell of pine, really, it just reminds me of those cheap little tree air fresheners they sell at the gas station down on 4th street... the one next to where that diner used to be before it closed down. They had really good pie there. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh. Yeah, even when I lay down to rest, it doesn't give me anything back. I just stay completely drained. I think my brother actually moved to Nevada after that lumber job...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. It's just... I wake up every single morning with this heavy, hopeless feeling right in the middle of my chest. Just weighing me down. I actually thought maybe it was the mattress at first, so I dragged it off the frame, but that didn't help. The frame is one of those heavy oak ones, you know? We bought it at an estate sale years ago. The guy selling it had this enormous collection of antique clocks. Must have been fifty of them ticking all at once in his living room, drove me absolutely crazy. I don't know how anyone could stand that much ticking, it was like being inside a time bomb. \n\nBut, um... anyway, the feeling in my chest. It gets so bad that some days I just go out and sit in the garage. I have this old folding chair out there, and I just sit in it and cry. I don't even know what started it half the time, I'm just sitting there crying by the lawnmower. The lawnmower needs a new spark plug, actually... I should probably write that down so I don't forget.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No... no, not really. I used to look forward to things. Like choir on Sundays, or playing pickup basketball with the guys. I used to love working on cars, too. I had this '68 Mustang I was restoring. Beautiful car, cherry red. I spent three years just trying to find the original carburetor for it. Found it at a swap meet down in Texas, actually. It was so hot that day, I swear my shoes were melting to the asphalt. We stopped at this little barbecue joint on the way back, best brisket I've ever had. I used to love grilling for the family on the weekends, making brisket... \n\nBut now... I don't know. I just can't make myself care anymore. I don't show up for choir, I haven't touched a wrench in months. The guys text me about basketball and I just... I don't even answer them. My phone just sits there buzzing. It's one of those new smartphones, my daughter bought it for me. It has a crack right down the middle of the screen because I dropped it in the driveway last Tuesday when it was raining...\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... gosh, about four months now, I think. Yeah, right around the time my wife went back to working full-time. She's a nurse, you know. Works over at the county hospital on the telemetry floor. They have her doing these twelve-hour shifts, which is just brutal on her feet. I bought her these special orthotic shoes for Christmas, the ones with the thick foam soles? The guy at the shoe store swore by them. He said his sister wore them when she worked at the post office. I don't know how postal workers do it, honestly, walking all those miles every single day, rain or shine. Our mail carrier, Dave, he's been delivering our route for fifteen years. He always leaves a little dog treat for our neighbor's golden retriever... \n\nBut, um... yeah, so she went back to work, and I started spending most of the weekdays just... alone in the house. Just me and the quiet. And it's been about four months of feeling like this since then. She used to work part-time at this little bakery downtown before that... they made the most incredible sourdough bread...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling nervous or anything like that. My neighbor, Tom, he gets nervous all the time, though. Especially about his property line. He's always out there checking the fence, making sure the neighborhood kids aren't leaning their bikes against it. He bought this expensive vinyl fencing last spring... bright white, supposed to never fade or chip. Reminds me of the old wooden picket fence my uncle used to have on his farm in Ohio. We used to go up there every summer when I was a kid. He grew the absolute best sweet corn... we'd boil it right after picking it. The water has to be at a rolling boil before you put the ears in, my aunt always said. She had this huge copper pot she used just for the corn. It was so heavy you had to use two hands just to lift it when it was empty. \n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Oh, nervous. No, I don't think I've been anxious. Just... just that heavy feeling I was talking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleeping? No, not really. I mean, I guess I sleep fine, or I don't really notice a problem. I usually go to bed around ten... well, it used to be ten. My wife always liked watching the late local news, channel 4. They have this meteorologist, Bob, he's been doing the weather there since the nineties. He always wears these ridiculous novelty ties. Like, on Thanksgiving, he'll wear a tie with little turkeys all over it. I don't know where he finds them. My dad had a tie like that once, but it was for fishing. Had little trout swimming up it. He loved fishing, my dad. We used to go up to Lake Michigan every August, rent this little cabin that smelled like pine needles and old damp wood. You could hear the loons out on the water right at dusk. It's an eerie sound, really, if you've never heard it. Almost sounds like a person laughing, but hollow. We'd sit on the porch and eat sunflower seeds...\n\nBut, uh, no, sleep. Sleep hasn't been an issue, I don't think. I just lay there and eventually I'm asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, I haven't really noticed a change, honestly. I guess I'm eating about the same. We usually have dinner around six, when my wife gets home. She likes to make these casseroles, you know? The ones where you throw everything into one dish and bake it. She got this new Pyrex dish a few weeks ago, one of those vintage-looking ones with the little blue flowers on the side. Reminds me of the dishes my grandmother used to have. She had this whole set of CorningWare, the ones with the little cornflower pattern. She used to make the best peach cobbler in those. We had this huge peach tree in the backyard when I was growing up. The peaches would get so ripe they'd just fall off the branch if you barely touched them. You had to eat them over the sink because the juice would just run everywhere. My brother and I used to have contests to see who could spit the pits the farthest. He always won, he had this gap in his front teeth that he said gave him better aerodynamics. He actually became an aerospace engineer, believe it or not. Works for Boeing now, out in Seattle. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he stands it. I always liked the sunshine, myself...\n\nBut, uh, food. Yeah, I think I'm eating normally. Nothing really different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Um... yeah. Actually, yeah. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. It's like this heavy loop in my head. And when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen. Being alone in the house... it's too quiet. We used to have this golden retriever, Buster, and he was never quiet. Always padding around, clicking his nails on the hardwood floors. We had those floors refinished about ten years ago. The guy who did it, Sal, he used this special polyurethane that was supposed to last twenty years without scratching. Sal was a character, always wore these suspenders with measuring tapes printed on them. Said his grandfather was a tailor in Brooklyn and wore the same kind. Brooklyn's changed so much, though. I saw a documentary on PBS the other night about how all the old neighborhoods are being gentrified. They were showing these artisanal mayonnaise shops... can you believe that? A whole store just for mayonnaise. My wife buys the store brand, the one with the yellow label. She says it tastes exactly the same as Hellmann's...\n\nBut, um, what was I... oh. Yeah, the thoughts. I just... I get scared I might actually do it when I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these thoughts of wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: How often? Um... it feels like it's almost all the time now. The thought just keeps coming, over and over, that I should just be dead. It's like a broken record. We used to have this old record player, a Zenith console from the sixties, the kind that looked like a piece of furniture. My parents kept it in the living room, and we weren't allowed to touch the needle. My dad had this huge collection of jazz records—Dave Brubeck, Miles Davis. He kept them in these pristine plastic sleeves. Whenever he put one on, he'd clean it first with this little velvet brush. He said dust was the enemy of good sound. Dust gets everywhere, though, doesn't it? No matter how much you clean. My wife is always dusting the ceiling fans. She bought this special extending duster just for it, the kind with the microfiber noodles on the end. It's bright orange. Reminds me of those safety vests construction workers wear. They’ve been doing construction on the highway near our house for three years now. The traffic is just awful, especially around rush hour. People just don't know how to merge anymore. They wait until the absolute last second and then just force their way in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. The thoughts. They just keep coming. And like I said, when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? Um... yeah. Actually, yes. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like... like my body is moving through mud. Just getting up from a chair feels like it takes all my energy. Mud is so heavy, you know? We used to go camping when I was a kid, up near Lake George, and one year it rained the entire week. The whole campsite turned into this thick, brown sludge. My dad's old station wagon—it was this huge Ford Country Squire with the fake wood paneling on the side—it got completely stuck. We had to wait for this guy with a tractor to pull us out. The tractor was an old John Deere, bright green and yellow. My neighbor two houses down just bought a riding mower that same color. He spends his entire Saturday out there, riding around in circles. He wears these big noise-canceling headphones, the kind they use at shooting ranges. I used to go to the range with my brother-in-law, Frank. Frank was a character, he collected antique coins. He had this one penny from 1909, an SVDB or something, he kept it in this little plastic case and treated it like it was the Holy Grail. People collect the strangest things, don't they? I saw a show once about people who collect vintage vacuum cleaners... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, moving slow. Yeah, it's not just moving, either. When people ask me something, I can hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. Like my brain is stuck in that mud, too.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Enjoy doing? Um... no. Not anymore. I used to, though. I used to really look forward to choir practice on Wednesdays, and playing pickup ball, working on cars in the garage... even just grilling out back for the family. But now... I just can't make myself care enough to show up for any of it. I don't even answer people's texts anymore. My phone just sits there buzzing. It's one of those new smartphones, the ones where the screen goes all the way to the edge. My daughter helped me set it up because I couldn't figure out how to transfer my contacts. She's good with technology, works in IT for a hospital downtown. The parking at that hospital is a nightmare, though. They have this huge parking garage, but the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your doors. Last time I was there, the guy next to me dinged my door. It was a brand new Honda, too. I always liked Hondas, they run forever if you change the oil on time. My first car was a Civic, a little hatchback. It was manual transmission, had a tape deck in it. I used to listen to those old cassette tapes until they got warped and sounded like they were underwater. You know how tapes used to get all tangled in the machine, and you had to wind them back up with a pencil? A number two pencil, always the yellow ones with the pink erasers. They don't make erasers like they used to, they just smudge everything now...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I just... I don't care about any of it anymore. I can't even text people back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, no, not restless. Actually, it's the exact opposite, like I was saying before. Everything I do has slowed way down. It's like my whole body is moving through thick mud. Mud... you know, my neighbor's dog got into some mud yesterday. It's a Golden Retriever, beautiful dog, but man, they shed everywhere. He brings it over sometimes and it just leaves this layer of blonde hair all over my porch. I tried sweeping it up with this new broom I bought at the hardware store. The hardware store down on 5th Street, the one with the creaky wooden floors. I love the smell of that place—sawdust and fertilizer. It reminds me of my grandfather's workshop. He used to build these intricate birdhouses out of cedar. Cedar is great because it naturally repels bugs. Bugs have been terrible this year, especially the mosquitoes. They say it's because of the mild winter we had. The snow barely stuck around for more than a day or two. I kind of missed the snow, to be honest. When I was a kid, we'd take these plastic sleds down the big hill behind the middle school... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Trouble sitting still? No, no. Like I said, getting up or moving just feels so heavy, like the mud. And it's not just my body. Even when someone asks me a question, I hear it fine, but... it just takes me such a long time to actually get the words out to answer them. Everything is just... slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, not at all. Like I was trying to explain, it's just the opposite. I don't have the energy to fidget. I'm just... stuck. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it feels exactly like I'm moving through mud. Mud... you know, they say mud baths are supposed to be good for your skin. My wife went to a spa once for her 40th birthday, and she came back smelling like sulfur. It was awful. She said it was relaxing, but I don't see how sitting in a tub of hot dirt could be relaxing. The spa was out in Sedona, Arizona. Have you ever been out there? The red rocks are beautiful, especially when the sun sets. We took a jeep tour while we were there, and the driver was this young kid, maybe twenty-two, who kept telling us all these conspiracy theories about aliens living under the mountains. He had this big bumper sticker on the back of the jeep that said \"The Truth is Out There.\" Reminded me of that old TV show, The X-Files. I used to watch that every Sunday night. The theme song always gave me the creeps. I tried to find it on streaming the other day, but there are so many streaming services now, it's impossible to keep track. You have to pay ten bucks here, fifteen bucks there, and pretty soon you're paying more than you did for cable. Cable used to be so simple. You just turned on the TV and flipped through the channels with a clicker... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Fidgeting? Right, no. I don't fidget. It's just... everything is so slow. Even when people ask me things, I hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. It's like my brain is stuck in the mud, too.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often these thoughts of wanting to be dead occur in a typical day?\n\nPatient: How often? Um... it's a lot. The thought just keeps coming that I should just be dead. Dead... you know, we drove past the old cemetery on Elm Street the other day. My wife was driving. They were doing some landscaping, cutting down these huge oak trees near the wrought iron fence. It's a shame, those trees must have been a hundred years old. My grandfather had an oak tree in his front yard. We used to rake the leaves into these massive piles in the fall and jump in them. Fall used to be my favorite season. The air gets so crisp, and you can finally wear a good sweater. I have this one heavy wool sweater my wife bought me in Ireland. We went there for our 20th anniversary. The flight was so long, I couldn't sleep at all. I tried to watch one of those movies they play on the little screen on the back of the seat, but the headphone jack was broken. It just kept cutting out. They don't even give you real headphones on planes anymore, just those cheap plastic earbuds that hurt your ears. My son goes through a pair of earbuds every month. He just leaves them in his pockets and they go right through the washing machine. You'd think he'd learn by now, but kids just don't pay attention to things... \n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? How often... right. Yeah, the thought just keeps coming that I should just be dead. And when I'm alone in the house, it really scares me. I sit there and I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't pace. I don't have the energy to pace. Like I've been trying to say, it's... it's all just so slow. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like my body is moving through mud. Mud... you know, my boots got covered in mud the last time it rained. I left them on the back porch and they're probably still out there, all caked in it. I bought those boots at a surplus store a few years ago. They're military grade, really heavy duty. The guy at the store said they'd last a lifetime. He was an older guy, had a patch on his jacket from the Vietnam War. My uncle served in Vietnam. He never really talked about it much, just said it was hot and rained all the time. Rain always makes my joints ache, especially my left knee. I tore a ligament playing softball in my thirties. We had a company team, the \"B-Shift Bombers.\" We weren't very good, but we always went out for pizza and beer after the games. There was this place called Luigi's that made the best deep dish. The crust was so thick you had to eat it with a knife and fork. I tried making pizza at home once, bought the dough and everything, but the oven got too hot and I burned the bottom black. The smoke alarm went off and scared the cat half to death. The cat hid under the sofa for three days. It's funny how animals react to loud noises...\n\nWait, pacing? No, no constant motion. I don't feel like moving at all. It's just... I'm moving through mud. And it's the same with talking. You ask me a question, and I hear it, I hear you perfectly fine, but... the words just won't come. It takes me a long time to get the words out. Everything is just so slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Restless? No... no, I don't feel restless. Like I keep trying to explain, I don't want to move at all. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it's just like my body is moving through mud. Mud... you know, my neighbor's dog tracked mud all over their new white carpets last week. It's a Golden Retriever, beautiful dog, but completely untrained. They just let him run loose in the yard, and he digs these massive holes near the fence line. I told them they should put up some chicken wire, but they didn't listen. People don't listen to advice anymore. Back when I worked at the hardware store, people used to come in all the time asking for advice on home projects. I'd spend twenty minutes explaining how to fix a leaky pipe or patch drywall. Drywall dust gets everywhere, you know. It coats your lungs if you don't wear a mask. I always wore one of those paper masks, the ones with the little metal strip you pinch over your nose. They always fogged up my glasses, though. I've worn glasses since I was twelve. I'm nearsighted. Without them, everything past my arm is just a big blur. I tried contacts once in my twenties, but I could never get them in my eyes right. Just the thought of touching my eyeball makes me cringe...\n\nWait, what was the question? Restless? Right, no. I'm not restless. I'm just... stuck in the mud. Even talking is hard. When people ask me something, I can hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. It's all just so slow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Keep moving? Doc, I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again. No, I don't need to keep moving. I don't want to do anything. Like I keep saying, everything I do is just slowed down. It's like my body is stuck in mud. Mud... you know, I got my truck stuck in the mud out by the old quarry a few years back. It was a '98 Chevy, terrible in the wet. You have to buff it out in little circles, otherwise it leaves streaks on the paint. Waxing a car takes a lot of elbow grease. My elbows have been popping lately whenever I bend my arms. Sounds like Rice Krispies. Snap, crackle, pop... I used to eat that for breakfast when I was a kid. You pour the milk in and just listen to it...

He bought this expensive vinyl fencing last spring... bright white, supposed to never fade or chip. Reminds me of the old wooden picket fence my uncle used to have on his farm in Ohio. We used to go up there every summer when I was a kid. He grew the absolute best sweet corn... we'd boil it right after picking it. Like, on Thanksgiving, he'll wear a tie with little turkeys all over it. I don't know where he finds them. My dad had a tie like that once, but it was for fishing. Had little trout swimming up it. He loved fishing, my dad. We used to go up to Lake Michigan every August, rent this little cabin that smelled like pine needles and old damp wood. You could hear the loons out on the water right at dusk. It's an eerie sound, really, if you've never heard it. Almost sounds like a person laughing, but hollow. Reminds me of the dishes my grandmother used to have. She had this whole set of CorningWare, the ones with the little cornflower pattern. She used to make the best peach cobbler in those. We had this huge peach tree in the backyard when I was growing up. The peaches would get so ripe they'd just fall off the branch if you barely touched them. You had to eat them over the sink because the juice would just run everywhere. My brother and I used to have contests to see who could spit the pits the farthest. He always won, he had this gap in his front teeth that he said gave him better aerodynamics. He actually became an aerospace engineer, believe it or not. Works for Boeing now, out in Seattle. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he stands it. I always liked the sunshine, myself...\n\nBut, uh, food. Yeah, I think I'm eating normally. Nothing really different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Um... yeah. Actually, yeah. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. It's like this heavy loop in my head. And when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen. Being alone in the house... it's too quiet. We used to have this golden retriever, Buster, and he was never quiet. Always padding around, clicking his nails on the hardwood floors. We had those floors refinished about ten years ago. The guy who did it, Sal, he used this special polyurethane that was supposed to last twenty years without scratching. Sal was a character, always wore these suspenders with measuring tapes printed on them. Said his grandfather was a tailor in Brooklyn and wore the same kind. Brooklyn's changed so much, though. I saw a documentary on PBS the other night about how all the old neighborhoods are being gentrified. They were showing these artisanal mayonnaise shops... can you believe that? A whole store just for mayonnaise. My wife buys the store brand, the one with the yellow label. She says it tastes exactly the same as Hellmann's...\n\nBut, um, what was I... oh. Yeah, the thoughts. I just... I get scared I might actually do it when I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these thoughts of wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: How often? Um... it feels like it's almost all the time now. The thought just keeps coming, over and over, that I should just be dead. It's like a broken record. We used to have this old record player, a Zenith console from the sixties, the kind that looked like a piece of furniture. My parents kept it in the living room, and we weren't allowed to touch the needle. My dad had this huge collection of jazz records—Dave Brubeck, Miles Davis. He kept them in these pristine plastic sleeves. Whenever he put one on, he'd clean it first with this little velvet brush. He said dust was the enemy of good sound. Dust gets everywhere, though, doesn't it? No matter how much you clean. My wife is always dusting the ceiling fans. She bought this special extending duster just for it, the kind with the microfiber noodles on the end. It's bright orange. Reminds me of those safety vests construction workers wear. They’ve been doing construction on the highway near our house for three years now. The traffic is just awful, especially around rush hour. People just don't know how to merge anymore. They wait until the absolute last second and then just force their way in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. The thoughts. They just keep coming. And like I said, when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? Um... yeah. Actually, yes. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like... like my body is moving through mud. Just getting up from a chair feels like it takes all my energy. Mud is so heavy, you know? We used to go camping when I was a kid, up near Lake George, and one year it rained the entire week. The whole campsite turned into this thick, brown sludge. My dad's old station wagon—it was this huge Ford Country Squire with the fake wood paneling on the side—it got completely stuck. We had to wait for this guy with a tractor to pull us out. The tractor was an old John Deere, bright green and yellow. My neighbor two houses down just bought a riding mower that same color. He spends his entire Saturday out there, riding around in circles. He wears these big noise-canceling headphones, the kind they use at shooting ranges. I used to go to the range with my brother-in-law, Frank. Frank was a character, he collected antique coins. He had this one penny from 1909, an SVDB or something, he kept it in this little plastic case and treated it like it was the Holy Grail. People collect the strangest things, don't they? I saw a show once about people who collect vintage vacuum cleaners... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, moving slow. Yeah, it's not just moving, either. When people ask me something, I can hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. Like my brain is stuck in that mud, too.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Enjoy doing? Um... no. Not anymore. I used to, though. I used to really look forward to choir practice on Wednesdays, and playing pickup ball, working on cars in the garage... even just grilling out back for the family. But now... I just can't make myself care enough to show up for any of it. I don't even answer people's texts anymore. My phone just sits there buzzing. It's one of those new smartphones, the ones where the screen goes all the way to the edge. My daughter helped me set it up because I couldn't figure out how to transfer my contacts. She's good with technology, works in IT for a hospital downtown. The parking at that hospital is a nightmare, though. They have this huge parking garage, but the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your doors. Last time I was there, the guy next to me dinged my door. It was a brand new Honda, too. I always liked Hondas, they run forever if you change the oil on time. My first car was a Civic, a little hatchback. It was manual transmission, had a tape deck in it. I used to listen to those old cassette tapes until they got warped and sounded like they were underwater. I can't even text people back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, no, not restless. Actually, it's the exact opposite, like I was saying before. Everything I do has slowed way down. It's like my whole body is moving through thick mud. Mud... you know, my neighbor's dog got into some mud yesterday. It's a Golden Retriever, beautiful dog, but man, they shed everywhere. He brings it over sometimes and it just leaves this layer of blonde hair all over my porch. I tried sweeping it up with this new broom I bought at the hardware store. The hardware store down on 5th Street, the one with the creaky wooden floors. I love the smell of that place—sawdust and fertilizer. It reminds me of my grandfather's workshop. He used to build these intricate birdhouses out of cedar. Cedar is great because it naturally repels bugs. Bugs have been terrible this year, especially the mosquitoes. They say it's because of the mild winter we had. The snow barely stuck around for more than a day or two. I kind of missed the snow, to be honest. When I was a kid, we'd take these plastic sleds down the big hill behind the middle school... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Trouble sitting still? No, no. Like I said, getting up or moving just feels so heavy, like the mud. And it's not just my body. Even when someone asks me a question, I hear it fine, but... it just takes me such a long time to actually get the words out to answer them. Everything is just... slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, not at all. Like I was trying to explain, it's just the opposite. I don't have the energy to fidget. I'm just... stuck. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it feels exactly like I'm moving through mud. Mud... you know, they say mud baths are supposed to be good for your skin. My wife went to a spa once for her 40th birthday, and she came back smelling like sulfur. It was awful. She said it was relaxing, but I don't see how sitting in a tub of hot dirt could be relaxing. The spa was out in Sedona, Arizona. Have you ever been out there? The red rocks are beautiful, especially when the sun sets. We took a jeep tour while we were there, and the driver was this young kid, maybe twenty-two, who kept telling us all these conspiracy theories about aliens living under the mountains. He had this big bumper sticker on the back of the jeep that said \"The Truth is Out There.\" Reminded me of that old TV show, The X-Files. I used to watch that every Sunday night. The theme song always gave me the creeps. I tried to find it on streaming the other day, but there are so many streaming services now, it's impossible to keep track. You have to pay ten bucks here, fifteen bucks there, and pretty soon you're paying more than you did for cable. Cable used to be so simple. He used to build these intricate birdhouses out of cedar. Cedar is great because it naturally repels bugs. Bugs have been terrible this year, especially the mosquitoes. They say it's because of the mild winter we had. The snow barely stuck around for more than a day or two. I kind of missed the snow, to be honest. When I was a kid, we'd take these plastic sleds down the big hill behind the middle school... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Trouble sitting still? No, no. Like I said, getting up or moving just feels so heavy, like the mud. And it's not just my body. Even when someone asks me a question, I hear it fine, but... it just takes me such a long time to actually get the words out to answer them. Everything is just... slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Fidgeting? No... no, not at all. Like I was trying to explain, it's just the opposite. I don't have the energy to fidget. I'm just... stuck. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it feels exactly like I'm moving through mud. Mud... you know, they say mud baths are supposed to be good for your skin. My wife went to a spa once for her 40th birthday, and she came back smelling like sulfur. It was awful. She said it was relaxing, but I don't see how sitting in a tub of hot dirt could be relaxing. The spa was out in Sedona, Arizona. Have you ever been out there? The red rocks are beautiful, especially when the sun sets. We took a jeep tour while we were there, and the driver was this young kid, maybe twenty-two, who kept telling us all these conspiracy theories about aliens living under the mountains. He had this big bumper sticker on the back of the jeep that said \"The Truth is Out There.\" Reminded me of that old TV show, The X-Files. I used to watch that every Sunday night. The theme song always gave me the creeps. I tried to find it on streaming the other day, but there are so many streaming services now, it's impossible to keep track. You have to pay ten bucks here, fifteen bucks there, and pretty soon you're paying more than you did for cable. Cable used to be so simple. You just turned on the TV and flipped through the channels with a clicker... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Fidgeting? Right, no. I don't fidget. It's just... everything is so slow. Even when people ask me things, I hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. It's like my brain is stuck in the mud, too.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often these thoughts of wanting to be dead occur in a typical day?\n\nPatient: How often? Um... it's a lot. The thought just keeps coming that I should just be dead. Dead... you know, we drove past the old cemetery on Elm Street the other day. My wife was driving. They were doing some landscaping, cutting down these huge oak trees near the wrought iron fence. It's a shame, those trees must have been a hundred years old. My grandfather had an oak tree in his front yard. We used to rake the leaves into these massive piles in the fall and jump in them. Fall used to be my favorite season. The air gets so crisp, and you can finally wear a good sweater. I have this one heavy wool sweater my wife bought me in Ireland. We went there for our 20th anniversary. The flight was so long, I couldn't sleep at all. I tried to watch one of those movies they play on the little screen on the back of the seat, but the headphone jack was broken. It just kept cutting out. They don't even give you real headphones on planes anymore, just those cheap plastic earbuds that hurt your ears. My son goes through a pair of earbuds every month. He just leaves them in his pockets and they go right through the washing machine. You'd think he'd learn by now, but kids just don't pay attention to things... \n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? How often... right. Yeah, the thought just keeps coming that I should just be dead. And when I'm alone in the house, it really scares me. I sit there and I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Pacing? No, I... I don't pace. I don't have the energy to pace. Like I've been trying to say, it's... it's all just so slow. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like my body is moving through mud. Mud... you know, my boots got covered in mud the last time it rained. I left them on the back porch and they're probably still out there, all caked in it. I bought those boots at a surplus store a few years ago. They're military grade, really heavy duty. The guy at the store said they'd last a lifetime. He was an older guy, had a patch on his jacket from the Vietnam War. My uncle served in Vietnam. He never really talked about it much, just said it was hot and rained all the time. Rain always makes my joints ache, especially my left knee. I tore a ligament playing softball in my thirties. We had a company team, the \"B-Shift Bombers.\" We weren't very good, but we always went out for pizza and beer after the games. There was this place called Luigi's that made the best deep dish. The crust was so thick you had to eat it with a knife and fork. I tried making pizza at home once, bought the dough and everything, but the oven got too hot and I burned the bottom black. The smoke alarm went off and scared the cat half to death. The cat hid under the sofa for three days. It's funny how animals react to loud noises...\n\nWait, pacing? No, no constant motion. I don't feel like moving at all. It's just... I'm moving through mud. And it's the same with talking. You ask me a question, and I hear it, I hear you perfectly fine, but... the words just won't come. It takes me a long time to get the words out. Everything is just so slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Restless? No... no, I don't feel restless. Like I keep trying to explain, I don't want to move at all. Everything I do has slowed down so much, it's just like my body is moving through mud. Mud... you know, my neighbor's dog tracked mud all over their new white carpets last week. It's a Golden Retriever, beautiful dog, but completely untrained. They just let him run loose in the yard, and he digs these massive holes near the fence line. I told them they should put up some chicken wire, but they didn't listen. People don't listen to advice anymore. Back when I worked at the hardware store, people used to come in all the time asking for advice on home projects. I'd spend twenty minutes explaining how to fix a leaky pipe or patch drywall. Drywall dust gets everywhere, you know. It coats your lungs if you don't wear a mask. I always wore one of those paper masks, the ones with the little metal strip you pinch over your nose. They always fogged up my glasses, though. I've worn glasses since I was twelve. I'm nearsighted. Without them, everything past my arm is just a big blur. I tried contacts once in my twenties, but I could never get them in my eyes right. Just the thought of touching my eyeball makes me cringe...\n\nWait, what was the question? Restless? Right, no. I'm not restless. I'm just... stuck in the mud. Even talking is hard. I had to wait three hours for a tow truck. The driver who finally showed up was eating a powdered donut, and he got white sugar dust all over my steering wheel. I hate a dirty car. I used to wash mine every Sunday morning, rain or shine. I'd use that special turtle wax, the kind that comes in the little green tub. You have to buff it out in little circles, otherwise it leaves streaks on the paint. Waxing a car takes a lot of elbow grease. My elbows have been popping lately whenever I bend my arms. Sounds like Rice Krispies. Snap, crackle, pop... I used to eat that for breakfast when I was a kid. You pour the milk in and just listen to it... \n\nWait, what did you just ask me? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Fidgeting? Doc, are we going in circles? I feel like you're not hearing me. No, I don't fidget. I don't have the energy to fidget. Like I've been trying to tell you, everything I do is incredibly slow. It's that mud feeling. Heavy, thick mud. Speaking of thick, my sister makes this gravy for Thanksgiving that is so thick it practically stands up on the spoon. She uses way too much cornstarch. Cornstarch is tricky, you have to mix it with cold water first to make a slurry, otherwise it gets all lumpy. Lumps in gravy are the worst. My grandmother used to make perfect gravy from the turkey drippings. She had this special gravy boat shaped like a little duck. I think it was porcelain. She kept it in a china cabinet that had these curved glass doors. Curved glass is really hard to replace if it breaks. You have to find a custom glassmaker. There used to be a glassblower downtown who made these little glass swans, he'd melt the glass with this huge torch. Fire has always fascinated me. If you stare into a campfire long enough, you start seeing shapes in the embers... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Fidgeting. Right. No. I sit perfectly still because moving is too hard. Even talking is a chore. You keep asking me these things, and I hear you, I hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me so long to gather the words and get them out to answer you. I'm not fidgeting. I'm just... completely slowed down.

I think my brother actually moved to Nevada after that lumber job...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. It's just... I wake up every single morning with this heavy, hopeless feeling right in the middle of my chest. Just weighing me down. I actually thought maybe it was the mattress at first, so I dragged it off the frame, but that didn't help. The frame is one of those heavy oak ones, you know? We bought it at an estate sale years ago. The guy selling it had this enormous collection of antique clocks. Must have been fifty of them ticking all at once in his living room, drove me absolutely crazy. I don't know how anyone could stand that much ticking, it was like being inside a time bomb. \n\nBut, um... anyway, the feeling in my chest. It gets so bad that some days I just go out and sit in the garage. I have this old folding chair out there, and I just sit in it and cry. I don't even know what started it half the time, I'm just sitting there crying by the lawnmower. The lawnmower needs a new spark plug, actually... I should probably write that down so I don't forget.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No... no, not really. I used to look forward to things. Like choir on Sundays, or playing pickup basketball with the guys. I used to love working on cars, too. I had this '68 Mustang I was restoring. Beautiful car, cherry red. I spent three years just trying to find the original carburetor for it. Found it at a swap meet down in Texas, actually. It was so hot that day, I swear my shoes were melting to the asphalt. We stopped at this little barbecue joint on the way back, best brisket I've ever had. I used to love grilling for the family on the weekends, making brisket... \n\nBut now... I don't know. I just can't make myself care anymore. I don't show up for choir, I haven't touched a wrench in months. The guys text me about basketball and I just... I don't even answer them. My phone just sits there buzzing. It's one of those new smartphones, my daughter bought it for me. It has a crack right down the middle of the screen because I dropped it in the driveway last Tuesday when it was raining...\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... gosh, about four months now, I think. Yeah, right around the time my wife went back to working full-time. She's a nurse, you know. Works over at the county hospital on the telemetry floor. They have her doing these twelve-hour shifts, which is just brutal on her feet. I bought her these special orthotic shoes for Christmas, the ones with the thick foam soles? The guy at the shoe store swore by them. He said his sister wore them when she worked at the post office. I don't know how postal workers do it, honestly, walking all those miles every single day, rain or shine. Our mail carrier, Dave, he's been delivering our route for fifteen years. He always leaves a little dog treat for our neighbor's golden retriever... \n\nBut, um... yeah, so she went back to work, and I started spending most of the weekdays just... alone in the house. Just me and the quiet. And it's been about four months of feeling like this since then. She used to work part-time at this little bakery downtown before that... they made the most incredible sourdough bread...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed feeling nervous or anything like that. My neighbor, Tom, he gets nervous all the time, though. Especially about his property line. He's always out there checking the fence, making sure the neighborhood kids aren't leaning their bikes against it. He bought this expensive vinyl fencing last spring... bright white, supposed to never fade or chip. Reminds me of the old wooden picket fence my uncle used to have on his farm in Ohio. We used to go up there every summer when I was a kid. He grew the absolute best sweet corn... we'd boil it right after picking it. The water has to be at a rolling boil before you put the ears in, my aunt always said. She had this huge copper pot she used just for the corn. It was so heavy you had to use two hands just to lift it when it was empty. \n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Oh, nervous. No, I don't think I've been anxious. Just... just that heavy feeling I was talking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleeping? No, not really. I mean, I guess I sleep fine, or I don't really notice a problem. I usually go to bed around ten... well, it used to be ten. My wife always liked watching the late local news, channel 4. They have this meteorologist, Bob, he's been doing the weather there since the nineties. He always wears these ridiculous novelty ties. Like, on Thanksgiving, he'll wear a tie with little turkeys all over it. I don't know where he finds them. My dad had a tie like that once, but it was for fishing. Had little trout swimming up it. He loved fishing, my dad. We used to go up to Lake Michigan every August, rent this little cabin that smelled like pine needles and old damp wood. You could hear the loons out on the water right at dusk. It's an eerie sound, really, if you've never heard it. Almost sounds like a person laughing, but hollow. We'd sit on the porch and eat sunflower seeds...\n\nBut, uh, no, sleep. Sleep hasn't been an issue, I don't think. I just lay there and eventually I'm asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, I haven't really noticed a change, honestly. I guess I'm eating about the same. We usually have dinner around six, when my wife gets home. She likes to make these casseroles, you know? The ones where you throw everything into one dish and bake it. She got this new Pyrex dish a few weeks ago, one of those vintage-looking ones with the little blue flowers on the side. Reminds me of the dishes my grandmother used to have. She had this whole set of CorningWare, the ones with the little cornflower pattern. She used to make the best peach cobbler in those. We had this huge peach tree in the backyard when I was growing up. The peaches would get so ripe they'd just fall off the branch if you barely touched them. You had to eat them over the sink because the juice would just run everywhere. My brother and I used to have contests to see who could spit the pits the farthest. He always won, he had this gap in his front teeth that he said gave him better aerodynamics. He actually became an aerospace engineer, believe it or not. Works for Boeing now, out in Seattle. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he stands it. I always liked the sunshine, myself...\n\nBut, uh, food. Yeah, I think I'm eating normally. Nothing really different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Um... yeah. Actually, yeah. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. It's like this heavy loop in my head. And when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen. Being alone in the house... it's too quiet. We used to have this golden retriever, Buster, and he was never quiet. Always padding around, clicking his nails on the hardwood floors. We had those floors refinished about ten years ago. The guy who did it, Sal, he used this special polyurethane that was supposed to last twenty years without scratching. Sal was a character, always wore these suspenders with measuring tapes printed on them. Said his grandfather was a tailor in Brooklyn and wore the same kind. Brooklyn's changed so much, though. I saw a documentary on PBS the other night about how all the old neighborhoods are being gentrified. They were showing these artisanal mayonnaise shops... can you believe that? A whole store just for mayonnaise. My wife buys the store brand, the one with the yellow label. She says it tastes exactly the same as Hellmann's...\n\nBut, um, what was I... oh. Yeah, the thoughts. I just... I get scared I might actually do it when I'm by myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have these thoughts of wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: How often? Um... it feels like it's almost all the time now. The thought just keeps coming, over and over, that I should just be dead. It's like a broken record. We used to have this old record player, a Zenith console from the sixties, the kind that looked like a piece of furniture. My parents kept it in the living room, and we weren't allowed to touch the needle. My dad had this huge collection of jazz records—Dave Brubeck, Miles Davis. He kept them in these pristine plastic sleeves. Whenever he put one on, he'd clean it first with this little velvet brush. He said dust was the enemy of good sound. Dust gets everywhere, though, doesn't it? No matter how much you clean. My wife is always dusting the ceiling fans. She bought this special extending duster just for it, the kind with the microfiber noodles on the end. It's bright orange. Reminds me of those safety vests construction workers wear. They’ve been doing construction on the highway near our house for three years now. The traffic is just awful, especially around rush hour. People just don't know how to merge anymore. They wait until the absolute last second and then just force their way in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. The thoughts. They just keep coming. And like I said, when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? Um... yeah. Actually, yes. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like... like my body is moving through mud. Just getting up from a chair feels like it takes all my energy. Mud is so heavy, you know? We used to go camping when I was a kid, up near Lake George, and one year it rained the entire week. The whole campsite turned into this thick, brown sludge. My dad's old station wagon—it was this huge Ford Country Squire with the fake wood paneling on the side—it got completely stuck. We had to wait for this guy with a tractor to pull us out. The tractor was an old John Deere, bright green and yellow. My neighbor two houses down just bought a riding mower that same color. He spends his entire Saturday out there, riding around in circles. He wears these big noise-canceling headphones, the kind they use at shooting ranges. I used to go to the range with my brother-in-law, Frank. Frank was a character, he collected antique coins. He had this one penny from 1909, an SVDB or something, he kept it in this little plastic case and treated it like it was the Holy Grail. People collect the strangest things, don't they? I saw a show once about people who collect vintage vacuum cleaners... \n\nBut, um, what were you asking? Oh, right, moving slow. Yeah, it's not just moving, either. When people ask me something, I can hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. Like my brain is stuck in that mud, too.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Enjoy doing? Um... no. Not anymore. I used to, though. I used to really look forward to choir practice on Wednesdays, and playing pickup ball, working on cars in the garage... even just grilling out back for the family. But now... I just can't make myself care enough to show up for any of it. I don't even answer people's texts anymore. My phone just sits there buzzing. It's one of those new smartphones, the ones where the screen goes all the way to the edge. My daughter helped me set it up because I couldn't figure out how to transfer my contacts. She's good with technology, works in IT for a hospital downtown. The parking at that hospital is a nightmare, though. They have this huge parking garage, but the spaces are so narrow you can barely open your doors. Last time I was there, the guy next to me dinged my door. It was a brand new Honda, too. I always liked Hondas, they run forever if you change the oil on time. My first car was a Civic, a little hatchback. It was manual transmission, had a tape deck in it. I used to listen to those old cassette tapes until they got warped and sounded like they were underwater. You know how tapes used to get all tangled in the machine, and you had to wind them back up with a pencil? A number two pencil, always the yellow ones with the pink erasers. They don't make erasers like they used to, they just smudge everything now...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. Yeah, no, I just... I don't care about any of it anymore. Actually, it's the exact opposite, like I was saying before. Everything I do has slowed way down. It's like my whole body is moving through thick mud. He brings it over sometimes and it just leaves this layer of blonde hair all over my porch. I tried sweeping it up with this new broom I bought at the hardware store. He used to build these intricate birdhouses out of cedar. Cedar is great because it naturally repels bugs. Bugs have been terrible this year, especially the mosquitoes. When I was a kid, we'd take these plastic sleds down the big hill behind the middle school... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Trouble sitting still? No, no. She said it was relaxing, but I don't see how sitting in a tub of hot dirt could be relaxing. The spa was out in Sedona, Arizona. Have you ever been out there? The red rocks are beautiful, especially when the sun sets. We took a jeep tour while we were there, and the driver was this young kid, maybe twenty-two, who kept telling us all these conspiracy theories about aliens living under the mountains. He had this big bumper sticker on the back of the jeep that said \"The Truth is Out There.\" Reminded me of that old TV show, The X-Files. I used to watch that every Sunday night. The theme song always gave me the creeps. I tried to find it on streaming the other day, but there are so many streaming services now, it's impossible to keep track. You have to pay ten bucks here, fifteen bucks there, and pretty soon you're paying more than you did for cable. Cable used to be so simple. You just turned on the TV and flipped through the channels with a clicker... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Fidgeting? Right, no. I don't fidget. It's just... everything is so slow. My grandfather had an oak tree in his front yard. We used to rake the leaves into these massive piles in the fall and jump in them. Fall used to be my favorite season. The air gets so crisp, and you can finally wear a good sweater. I have this one heavy wool sweater my wife bought me in Ireland. We went there for our 20th anniversary. He was an older guy, had a patch on his jacket from the Vietnam War. My uncle served in Vietnam. He never really talked about it much, just said it was hot and rained all the time. The crust was so thick you had to eat it with a knife and fork. I tried making pizza at home once, bought the dough and everything, but the oven got too hot and I burned the bottom black. The smoke alarm went off and scared the cat half to death. The cat hid under the sofa for three days. It's funny how animals react to loud noises...\n\nWait, pacing? No, no constant motion. I don't feel like moving at all. It's just... I'm moving through mud. And it's the same with talking. It's all just so slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling like you need to keep moving or doing something when you're not feeling slowed down?\n\nPatient: Keep moving? You have to buff it out in little circles, otherwise it leaves streaks on the paint. Waxing a car takes a lot of elbow grease. My elbows have been popping lately whenever I bend my arms. Sounds like Rice Krispies. Snap, crackle, pop... I used to eat that for breakfast when I was a kid. You pour the milk in and just listen to it... \n\nWait, what did you just ask me? She kept it in a china cabinet that had these curved glass doors. Curved glass is really hard to replace if it breaks. You have to find a custom glassmaker. You could hear the loons out on the water right at dusk. It's an eerie sound, really, if you've never heard it. Almost sounds like a person laughing, but hollow. I just lay there and eventually I'm asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, I haven't really noticed a change, honestly. Reminds me of the dishes my grandmother used to have. She had this whole set of CorningWare, the ones with the little cornflower pattern. She used to make the best peach cobbler in those. We had this huge peach tree in the backyard when I was growing up. The peaches would get so ripe they'd just fall off the branch if you barely touched them. You had to eat them over the sink because the juice would just run everywhere. My brother and I used to have contests to see who could spit the pits the farthest. He always won, he had this gap in his front teeth that he said gave him better aerodynamics. He actually became an aerospace engineer, believe it or not. Works for Boeing now, out in Seattle. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he stands it. I always liked the sunshine, myself...\n\nBut, uh, food. Yeah, I think I'm eating normally. Nothing really different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Um... yeah. Actually, yeah. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. It's like this heavy loop in my head. The guy who did it, Sal, he used this special polyurethane that was supposed to last twenty years without scratching. Sal was a character, always wore these suspenders with measuring tapes printed on them. Said his grandfather was a tailor in Brooklyn and wore the same kind. Brooklyn's changed so much, though. I saw a documentary on PBS the other night about how all the old neighborhoods are being gentrified. They were showing these artisanal mayonnaise shops... can you believe that? A whole store just for mayonnaise. My wife buys the store brand, the one with the yellow label. She says it tastes exactly the same as Hellmann's...\n\nBut, um, what was I... oh. Yeah, the thoughts. Um... yeah. Actually, yes. Everything I do has slowed down. It's like... like my body is moving through mud. Just getting up from a chair feels like it takes all my energy. Mud is so heavy, you know? We used to go camping when I was a kid, up near Lake George, and one year it rained the entire week. The whole campsite turned into this thick, brown sludge. My dad's old station wagon—it was this huge Ford Country Squire with the fake wood paneling on the side—it got completely stuck. We had to wait for this guy with a tractor to pull us out. The tractor was an old John Deere, bright green and yellow. My neighbor two houses down just bought a riding mower that same color. He spends his entire Saturday out there, riding around in circles. He wears these big noise-canceling headphones, the kind they use at shooting ranges. I used to go to the range with my brother-in-law, Frank. Frank was a character, he collected antique coins. He had this one penny from 1909, an SVDB or something, he kept it in this little plastic case and treated it like it was the Holy Grail. People collect the strangest things, don't they? When people ask me something, I can hear the question perfectly fine, but... it just takes me a really long time to get the words out. I just can't make myself care enough to show up for any of it. I don't even answer people's texts anymore. My phone just sits there buzzing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pacing? Tapping my feet? Doc, I... I don't know how else to say this. I feel like a broken record. No. No pacing, no tapping. I don't have the energy for any of that. I'm telling you, everything I do has just slowed down to a crawl. It's exactly like I said, like my body is moving through deep, thick mud. Speaking of mud, my boots are still out on the porch from the last time it rained. They're those rubber Wellington boots, the green ones. They're great for keeping your feet dry, but they have zero arch support. My arches have been falling for years. The podiatrist told me I needed custom orthotics, but they wanted four hundred dollars for them. Four hundred dollars for two pieces of plastic you shove in your shoes! It's highway robbery. Just like the price of groceries these days. I went to get a carton of eggs last week, and they were almost six dollars. Six dollars! And they weren't even the fancy organic ones, just regular white eggs. I used to like my eggs sunny-side up, with a piece of toast to dip in the yolk. You have to use a good, sturdy bread for that, like a sourdough or a thick rye. Rye bread always has those little caraway seeds in it. I don't really like seeds getting stuck in my teeth. I had a popcorn kernel stuck in my gums for three days once, it drove me absolutely crazy...

Hold on, what did you ask me? About pacing? Right. No. I'm not doing more physical activity. It's the exact opposite. Like I've been trying to explain... when you ask me these things, I can hear the question just fine, but... it's just taking me such a long time to get the words out to tell you. I'm not moving more. I'm barely moving at all.

Doc, I... I really don't know how many different ways I can say this. Are you writing this down? Because I feel like I'm just repeating myself over and over. No. I am not doing more physical activity. I'm not doing *any* physical activity if I can help it. 

I keep telling you, everything I do is just... it's slowed down. It's like I'm dragging my body through a swamp of thick mud. Swamp mud... that reminds me of this documentary I watched on the Everglades. They have these huge airboats that just glide right over the water and the grass. The fans on the back are incredibly loud, you have to wear those big ear protection muffs like the guys on the airport tarmac wear. I flew to Denver once, back in '05. The turbulence over the Rockies was awful. The guy sitting next to me spilled his tomato juice all over his khakis. Tomato stains are impossible to get out. You have to use club soda and dab it, never rub. My mother taught me that. She was a fanatic about laundry, she used to hang everything out on the line in the backyard. The sheets always smelled like sunshine and fresh air. You can't get that smell from a dryer sheet, no matter what the commercials say. Those commercials are always so fake anyway, with the actors smiling while they do chores. Who smiles while doing laundry? 

Wait... what was the question? Physical activity. Right. No. I'm not moving more. I'm barely moving. Even this... talking to you. I hear you ask the question, I understand what you're saying perfectly fine, but... it's just taking me forever to actually get the words to come out of my mouth. I'm just... I'm just so slow.

He's always out there checking the fence, making sure the neighborhood kids aren't leaning their bikes against it. He bought this expensive vinyl fencing last spring... bright white, supposed to never fade or chip. Reminds me of the old wooden picket fence my uncle used to have on his farm in Ohio. We used to go up there every summer when I was a kid. He grew the absolute best sweet corn... we'd boil it right after picking it. The water has to be at a rolling boil before you put the ears in, my aunt always said. She had this huge copper pot she used just for the corn. I mean, I guess I sleep fine, or I don't really notice a problem. I usually go to bed around ten... well, it used to be ten. My wife always liked watching the late local news, channel 4. They have this meteorologist, Bob, he's been doing the weather there since the nineties. He always wears these ridiculous novelty ties. Like, on Thanksgiving, he'll wear a tie with little turkeys all over it. I don't know where he finds them. My dad had a tie like that once, but it was for fishing. Had little trout swimming up it. He loved fishing, my dad. We used to go up to Lake Michigan every August, rent this little cabin that smelled like pine needles and old damp wood. You could hear the loons out on the water right at dusk. It's an eerie sound, really, if you've never heard it. Almost sounds like a person laughing, but hollow. We'd sit on the porch and eat sunflower seeds...\n\nBut, uh, no, sleep. Sleep hasn't been an issue, I don't think. I just lay there and eventually I'm asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, I haven't really noticed a change, honestly. I guess I'm eating about the same. We usually have dinner around six, when my wife gets home. She likes to make these casseroles, you know? The ones where you throw everything into one dish and bake it. She got this new Pyrex dish a few weeks ago, one of those vintage-looking ones with the little blue flowers on the side. Reminds me of the dishes my grandmother used to have. She had this whole set of CorningWare, the ones with the little cornflower pattern. She used to make the best peach cobbler in those. We had this huge peach tree in the backyard when I was growing up. The peaches would get so ripe they'd just fall off the branch if you barely touched them. You had to eat them over the sink because the juice would just run everywhere. My brother and I used to have contests to see who could spit the pits the farthest. He always won, he had this gap in his front teeth that he said gave him better aerodynamics. He actually became an aerospace engineer, believe it or not. Works for Boeing now, out in Seattle. It rains so much out there, I don't know how he stands it. I always liked the sunshine, myself...\n\nBut, uh, food. Yeah, I think I'm eating normally. Nothing really different.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wanting to be dead?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? Um... yeah. Actually, yeah. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. It's like this heavy loop in my head. And when I'm alone, it really scares me because I start thinking I might actually do something to make that happen. Being alone in the house... it's too quiet. We used to have this golden retriever, Buster, and he was never quiet. Always padding around, clicking his nails on the hardwood floors. We had those floors refinished about ten years ago. The guy who did it, Sal, he used this special polyurethane that was supposed to last twenty years without scratching. Sal was a character, always wore these suspenders with measuring tapes printed on them. Said his grandfather was a tailor in Brooklyn and wore the same kind. Brooklyn's changed so much, though. I saw a documentary on PBS the other night about how all the old neighborhoods are being gentrified. They were showing these artisanal mayonnaise shops... can you believe that?
